          1                      P R O C E E D I N G S

          2              MR. VALDEZ:  Good morning, everybody.  My name is

          3   Alandro Valdez.  I am from the Commission's Office of

          4   Technical Reporting and Economics.

          5              We are pleased to welcome you to this

          6   Commissioner-led technical conference to discuss PJM's

          7   governance and stakeholder processes.

          8              The purpose of this technical conference is to

          9   explore specific governance features and stakeholder

         10   processes in PJM that affect timely actions on

         11   operational and market needs.  The conference will also

         12   explore potential reforms to PJM's governance and

         13   stakeholder processes.

         14              Before we begin with opening remarks from the

         15   Chairman and the Commissioners, I will outline some

         16   logistics for the conference.  

         17              This technical conference will run from 9:00

         18   a.m. to 4:00 p.m. today.  We will have opening remarks from

         19   invited speakers in the first session, followed by two

         20   panels, with three slates of panelists.

         21              We will start with our opening speakers, followed

         22   by the first panel, on the PJM governance structure.  This

         23   will be followed by a second panel, on PJM's stakeholder

         24   processes, which will have two slates.

         25              We will have a break between each panel, one hour





                                                                        7



          1   for lunch after the first panel and a 15-minute break

          2   between the two second panels.

          3              Only the commissioners, panelists, and a small

          4   group of Commission staff will have speaking roles during

          5   the conference.  We will not be taking any questions from

          6   the audience.

          7              The conference is being webcast and transcribed

          8   and a recording will be available for future viewing.

          9              Commenters are also invited to file

         10   post-conference comments in the docket, AD26-7, following

         11   the conference.

         12              For those standing in the back of the room, we

         13   invite you to go to hearing room one, for overflow seating,

         14   where the conference is being livestreamed from here.

         15              As a reminder for all panelists, we will not

         16   discuss at this technical conference any specific proceeding

         17   before the Commission, including proceedings that involve

         18   similar issues, whether listed on the supplemental notice

         19   issued July 16th, or not.

         20              We ask that panelists, similarly, refrain from

         21   such discussion.  If anyone engages in these kinds of

         22   discussions, my colleagues from the Office of the General

         23   Counsel will interrupt the discussion to ask the speaker to

         24   avoid that topic.

         25              With those initial matters out of the way, I'll





                                                                        8



          1   now turn it over to Chairman Swett for her opening remarks.

          2              CHAIRMAN SWETT:  Thank you very much.  Good

          3   morning, everyone.  I don't think we've ever been so

          4   popular.  It's probably because of our guest speakers to my

          5   left, but it's probably more likely due to the gravity of

          6   the situation.  Thank you all for being here.

          7              I want to begin by grounding us in why we're

          8   convened today and why this conversation is so urgent and

          9   necessary.  As many of you know, at PJM's annual meeting in

         10   Baltimore this past May I remarked on the history of the

         11   market nearly a century ago when it formed and the brave

         12   leadership and oversight that -- of the foresight of the

         13   utilities that banded together in 1927 to form a power pool

         14   that produced benefits greater than any one of them could

         15   achieve individually.

         16              For many decades, that spirit drove PJM to be a

         17   leader in pursuing innovative market designs and operating

         18   one of the largest, most complex, transmission systems in

         19   the world.  We appreciate, at this commission, how difficult

         20   that task is and how it has grown increasingly difficult

         21   today, as PJM sits at the crossroads of 13 states and the

         22   District of Columbia, and there are divergent regulatory

         23   structures, resource portfolios, and politics, across the

         24   footprint.  It's a very difficult needle to thread.

         25              But we're here today because PJM is facing a





                                                                        9



          1   grave legitimacy crisis, frustrations are mounting, the

          2   collaborative spirit that underpinned the market's formation

          3   is fracturing, and some transmission owners are openly

          4   discussing leaving the RTO altogether.  Put plainly, market

          5   participants have lost confidence in PJM's decision-making

          6   abilities.

          7              But we are not here to cast blame today or point

          8   fingers or simply just air grievances.  I convened this

          9   conference today for one purpose.  That is to right the

         10   ship.  We are at a global inflection point, where nations

         11   are competing with the United States to harness the power of

         12   AI in ways that will reshape economies and geopolitics, for

         13   our lifetimes and generations after that.

         14              Our adversaries are hard at work and it's time

         15   that the companies and institutions represented in this room

         16   come together to chart the path towards a well-functioning

         17   RTO that can deliver for the American people.

         18              As I have said before, PJM is the tip of the

         19   spear, it's the laboratory of national and economic security on

         20   which our country may rise or fall.  And I'm not being

         21   dramatic; I truly believe that, and it is critical that we

         22   come out of today's conference with solutions.

         23              We need PJM to act decisively and reinstill the

         24   confidence in investors that is necessary for the region to

         25   attract the billions in capital that we need to build





                                                                       10



          1   generation and harden our infrastructure.  To do that, we

          2   have to fix the cracks in the foundational governance

          3   structure.

          4              I want PJM to be set up for success as this

          5   country navigates once-in-a-generation opportunities to

          6   cement American leadership in the global economy.

          7              As we consider potential reforms today, we can't

          8   lose sight of the 67 million Americans who directly feel the

          9   consequences of our actions on their pocketbooks. 

         10   Hard-working American families have enough on their plates

         11   and the price of electricity cannot be another source of

         12   strain.  Families in the PJM footprint should not be asked

         13   to shoulder the financial burden of processes that are

         14   slow, fragmented, or opaque.

         15              I define success as a well-functioning RTO that

         16   delivers stable, transparent, and cost-effective outcomes. 

         17   Today we begin the process of identifying and evaluating

         18   actionable reforms that will allow PJM to operate with

         19   speed, transparency, and accountability.

         20              Here is what I ask of everyone participating

         21   today.  First, be candid.  If a rule or process is failing,

         22   say so.  If things need to be eliminated, streamlined, or

         23   strengthened, we want to hear why.  We want specifics and we

         24   want the truth.

         25              Second, on the point of specificity, I personally





                                                                       11



          1   despise equivocations and hollow pledges.  We are looking

          2   for the reforms that are clearly articulated, evaluated, and

          3   we want them to be filed under Section 205, or Section 206,

          4   as appropriate.  If there are roadblocks to turning those

          5   reforms into realities, I want to hear what those are and

          6   how to cut through them.

          7              Third, I ask that each company and institution

          8   approach today's conversation with an eye towards reaching

          9   common ground.  This is not the time to fall back on

         10   familiar positions that have been worn over time and decades

         11   of businesses.  Reaffirming immovable stances is not

         12   productive.  We need change.  The market is facing change,

         13   and we're not going to succeed without it.  That means that

         14   you need to listen as much as you speak and acknowledge

         15   valid concerns, even when they come from a different side of

         16   the table.

         17              It also means looking at practical compromises

         18   and being willing to adjust longstanding positions, for the

         19   benefit of the 67 million Americans who are depending on us

         20   to get this right.

         21              And, if we can't all agree, then FERC will be in

         22   a position where we may have to do it for you.  And I would

         23   rather not be in that position.

         24              So... we will not discuss at this technical

         25   conference any specific proceedings before the Commission, including





                                                                       12



          1   proceedings that involve similar issues, whether listed on

          2   the supplemental notice or not, and we ask that the

          3   panelists similarly refrain from such discussions.  If

          4   anyone engages in these types of discussions, my colleagues

          5   from the Office of General Counsel will interrupt the

          6   discussion to ask the speaker to avoid that topic.

          7              With those initial matters out of the way, I will

          8   turn to my colleagues for their opening remarks. 

          9   Commissioner Rosner.

         10              COMMISSIONER ROSNER:  Thank you, Chairman, for

         11   convening this important conference and thank you to the

         12   panelists that will be appearing today for being here and

         13   contributing your perspective.  

         14              Thanks to everyone who filed comments, and thank

         15   you to Secretary Danly and to Peter Lake for joining us.

         16              And I'd be remiss if I didn't also thank the FERC

         17   staff for organizing the conference.

         18              Yes, PJM has problems.  Yes, they should be

         19   fixed, and it's urgent to do so.  But I want to take a

         20   moment to just acknowledge that, since it was created, PJM

         21   has saved consumers billions of dollars, year after year. 

         22   PJM has also ensured that the grid has remained reliable. 

         23   And this has been particularly evident over the last few

         24   years, when PJM has managed challenging conditions, to keep

         25   the lights on.





                                                                       13



          1              So, while we're convened here today to discuss

          2   changes to PJM to ensure that the nation's largest energy

          3   market remains sustainable and viable in the coming years,

          4   it's also important not to lose sight of PJM's importance

          5   and value.

          6              So, turning to the topic at hand, effective

          7   governance processes, are critical, for PJM to continue

          8   delivering for the region.  My hope is that today we build

          9   consensus on durable, lasting, reforms, and I'm encouraged

         10   by the hard work of reaching agreement that has already

         11   begun in earnest, and I'm optimistic that we can continue

         12   moving that ball forward today, because the opportunity

         13   before us today is one we can't afford not to capitalize on.

         14              I'm approaching today's conversation with an open

         15   mind.  That said, I'd like to offer a few opening thoughts

         16   on five categories of potential reforms that stakeholders

         17   have discussed with me, both in meetings in person and in

         18   comments.

         19              First, I agree that states need an enhanced role

         20   in the governance process, and this must be a real role,

         21   with teeth.  For example, some form of increased state

         22   participation in selecting the board sounds very reasonable,

         23   appreciating that there are many different pathways to

         24   achieve this.

         25              Second, I see support for enhanced board





                                                                       14



          1   independence.  To succeed, the PJM board needs to be able to

          2   make decisions confidently and openly, and decisively act

          3   once it's reached the best possible decision.  Achieving

          4   this outcome also can come in many different forms.

          5              Third, I'm interested in discussing pathways,

          6   either in the stakeholder process or in another venue, to

          7   amplify state input on issues related to resource adequacy. 

          8   Under the Federal Power Act, jurisdiction between the

          9   federal government and states is shared.  States are

         10   critical partners in ensuring resource adequacy, because

         11   states are ultimately responsible for siting and permitting

         12   the generation and transmission that is needed to ensure

         13   reliable and affordable power.  And I'll have more on this

         14   later.

         15              In this area, I hear a variety of potential

         16   solutions, ranging from concepts like we have in other RTOs,

         17   where state entities have opportunities to present issues

         18   directly to the board, as well as processes like the one

         19   that we set up in Order Number 1920, where we required public

         20   utilities to tell FERC what states agreed to on

         21   tranmission cost allocation.

         22              And that takes me to the fourth area of focus,

         23   state input on transmission cost allocation.  In my view,

         24   the expectation, the norm, should always be that states and

         25   their utilities agree on how to allocate the cost of





                                                                       15



          1   transmission such that a process like the one we established

          2   in Order Number 1920 is never needed.

          3              And, on that Order Number 1920 cost allocation in

          4   PJM, I'm optimistic that we will get that agreement.  But

          5   the point here is that states should always be able to

          6   formally communicate to PJM, and to FERC, in turn, what they

          7   agreed to.

          8              Fifth, I also believe it's important to have an

          9   entity for the governors' designees, and with this they can

         10   ensure -- we should ensure that they have the resources

         11   needed to organize themselves and their positions.

         12              Next, as we consider PJM governance in the

         13   stakeholder process and particularly a meaningful,

         14   potentially expanded, role for the states, very

         15   respectfully, states must also ensure that they make

         16   progress on the issues within their own jurisdictions,

         17   because I mentioned before, it's shared.

         18              For example, we need states' help with retail

         19   rate design, to ensure that they can assign wholesale costs

         20   to the right customers.  We also need states' help on

         21   curtailment priorities, otherwise known as emergency load

         22   reduction rules, for large loads.  And we need states to put

         23   in place cost allocation rules for a backstop auction, if and

         24   when we have one.

         25              And all of this, PJM state governors committed to





                                                                       16



          1   doing this backstop cost allocation when they signed the

          2   "statement of principles" regarding PJM agreement, in

          3   January, with the White House, and it's time for these

          4   provisions to be in place across the region.

          5              I also note that the Virginia SCC and the Data

          6   Center Coalition, a state and industry working together,

          7   proposed both backstop cost allocation and curtailment

          8   rules, in June, and I hope that the rest of the PJM states

          9   will join, and provide some place where we can have

         10   transparent information on the status of their member

         11   states' progress on these critical deliverables.

         12              And I wouldn't be doing my job here if I didn't

         13   also mention that we need states' help on permitting.  No

         14   matter what signal the PJM market sends or what governance

         15   reforms are implemented, PJM will not succeed without our state

         16   colleagues also ensuring that we timely get generation and

         17   transmission built.  We need help from states to make sure

         18   that they can identify resources they want to see built in

         19   their state, and then ensure that those resources get built

         20   and connected fast.

         21              For example, if your states has clean energy

         22   goals, ask yourself, "How long does it take to get a permit,

         23   the CPCN, all the authorizations to build a battery?"  If

         24   it's longer than 60 days, that sounds like a problem.  If

         25   you don't know the answer, to me that sounds like a problem





                                                                       17



          1   too.

          2              If your state wants to embrace demand response

          3   and demand side flexibility, how much retail demand response

          4   currently participates in your state?  Does your state allow

          5   aggregators access to retail meter data?  Do your utilities

          6   allow negative peak load contribution values, to enable

          7   innovative business models that can provide real RA during

          8   stress periods?

          9              And finally, on transmission, we all know that

         10   transmission is needed, to interconnect new resources.  We

         11   can have the fastest queue in the world, but if there's not a

         12   grid to plug it in there is nothing we can do fast.  So, how

         13   quickly and durably can transmission be developed in your

         14   state?

         15              So, thank you for humoring my request for

         16   assistance from our state colleagues.  While the Commission

         17   works to make progress on issues within our jurisdiction, we

         18   really need PJM, the market participants, states and other

         19   stakeholders, to make progress on these issues that are

         20   beyond our reach.

         21              But together I'm very confident that we can

         22   address the challenges we face, and I remain optimistic

         23   about PJM's future.

         24              With that, I'm very much looking forward to the

         25   conversation today and I turn it back to you, Chairman.





                                                                       18



          1              CHAIRMAN SWETT:  That was a nice balance to my

          2   brimstone.  Commissioner See?

          3              COMMISSIONER SEE:  There are tech conference and

          4   there are tech conferences, and I think it's obvious from

          5   the people who are here today and the attention that's been

          6   placed on this issue that we're in the latter category.

          7              First of all, a well-deserved round of thanks. 

          8   The prep work and the background for conversations like I

          9   hope we'll have today don't just happen.  So, thank you,

         10   Chair Swett, and to your office for bringing us together and

         11   focusing on these issues.  Thank you to our FERC staff for

         12   all of their work here, to PJM for being here and for

         13   helpful conversations along the way.

         14              To our distinguished guests and our panelists, I appreciate

         15   your willingness to put your name to ideas and engage with

         16   us on that as well.  And to the many other commenters who

         17   have already given us a strong record to start from.

         18              We have a lot to do today, so here's how I see

         19   the problem statement.  PJM, like other organized markets,

         20   has delivered huge value to customers over the years.  When

         21   functioning right, competitive markets and the economies of

         22   scale that can come with them can mean lower-cost power and

         23   better operational reliability for millions of Americans.

         24              But PJM hasn't been living up to that legacy

         25   recently.  Even with market intervention, we still have





                                                                       19



          1   sky-high prices, and last week was the second time that the

          2   auction failed to secure enough capacity to meet the reserve

          3   threshold in PJM.  To put it lightly, those are serious

          4   concerns.

          5              Now, to be clear, some of the big factors for

          6   this are outside of PJM's control.  Markets are designed to

          7   withstand some fluctuation, but the pace and scale of large

          8   load growth is well beyond an ordinary demand spike.

          9              So, I don't see the need for reforms as the

         10   problem itself.  For these conditions, we need real changes

         11   in the short term, to get us through this time and to allow

         12   PJM to keep delivering benefits into the future.

         13              As I do see it, the problem is twofold.  First,

         14   PJM's unique stakeholder process and system of voting may

         15   work in many contexts, but it hasn't led to results in some

         16   of the most consequential questions PJM is grappling with

         17   now.  We need action, not stalemates.

         18              And second, as a result of these and other

         19   struggles, states, utilities, developers, and other

         20   stakeholders have lost confidence in PJM.  Either of these

         21   are serious issues on their own, but together they put PJM

         22   at a crisis point.

         23              And that's why governance reform and the

         24   conversation today matter.  It's because I take so seriously

         25   the substantive challenges PJM has had that we have to





                                                                       20



          1   address how to get governance right.  PJM needs a process

          2   and a structure that can get these reforms done and can give

          3   stakeholders confidence enough in them to bet their money

          4   and their customers on the results.

          5              So I think today we need more than just

          6   conversation.  I'm really hoping to see us flesh out viable

          7   options and see where there is, or can, or should be

          8   consensus, moving forward.

          9              We've had a lot of strong ideas teed up already

         10   and I'm really grateful for that.  Some feel like they could

         11   be low hanging fruit or areas where we should be able to get

         12   easier momentum.

         13              For me, that category includes ways to make the

         14   board more transparent and accountable, so ideas like

         15   lengthening board terms, reevaluating selection criteria and

         16   conflict of interest rules, to get more members in the PJM

         17   region and others with direct experience and investment,

         18   consistent with good governance principles.

         19              I also think we can talk, in this category, about

         20   ways to increase board members' interaction with states and

         21   stakeholders and to have more transparency around board

         22   decisions.  So let's talk about these and other areas, where

         23   we may be able to get some relatively fast wins.

         24              But, there are other areas, of course, where I

         25   acknowledge, as we all do, it'll be harder to get reforms in





                                                                       21



          1   place.  But that's all the more reason we have to talk about

          2   them here, with the breadth of interests and expertise

          3   represented, and the urgency of the stakes.

          4              So I'm very interested in discussions about how

          5   to give states a more meaningful voice in PJM and in selecting its

          6   leadership.  State representation on the nominating

          7   committee seems an obvious step forward, for me, in this

          8   area, and I'm also interested in talking about ways to

          9   strengthen OPSI or expand the states' formal mechanisms to

         10   help set PJM's direction.

         11              And I'm very focused on how we can make the

         12   stakeholder process more effective and let PJM act more

         13   decisively.  So, let's not shy away, today, from whether an

         14   advisory stakeholder process, which is almost unique from

         15   other markets, is the right path forward for PJM.  That

         16   would undoubtedly be a big change, and I do have an open

         17   mind on this issue.

         18              But, paired with jump ball filing rights and

         19   similar 4C mechanisms for collaboration, it could be a

         20   method that lets PJM act in a faster and more decisive way

         21   than it can now.

         22              So, I'm interested in hearing from the panelists

         23   why that is, or isn't, the path forward, and what it would

         24   take to get there.

         25              Now to be clear, in closing -- and I have respect





                                                                       22



          1   for PJM, especially when I talk with the technical staff,

          2   it's clear that they are approaching the generational issues

          3   in front of us, with nuance and thoughtfulness.  But we need

          4   more.

          5              As a PJM ratepayer and an American, I want PJM to

          6   succeed.  As a FERC commissioner, I also take seriously our

          7   regulatory responsibilities to make sure the market operates

          8   in a just and reasonable way.  

          9              So, success requires action, and let's work

         10   together today to make sure that that can spur it on.  Back to you.

         11              CHAIRMAN SWETT:  Thank you.  Commissioner Chang?

         24   exercise.

         25              I also want to acknowledge that PJM has

          1   successfully -- and we talked about benefits, my colleagues

          6   that was designed today, over 30 -- about 39 gigawatts of

          7   just combined cycle.  I'm not even talking about the other

         14              But recently, I think, the resource adequacy has

         15   been a tremendous concern for everyone, and while today's

         16   governance discussion will not address all of the problems

         17   and all the resource adequacy concerns, I think this is a

         18   very important component of this.

         19              I also want to acknowledge that PJM has been

         20   relatively responsive to states, in the past, particularly

         21   when certain states have specific needs, and studies.  And I

         22   was working in New England when I jealously looked at PJM

         23   and its staff, working with certain states in the PJM

         24   footprint, to help them facilitate their needs.

                                                                       24



          1   forget the challenges that PJM has overcome over the past

          2   decade, so I appreciate the interest today and over the last

          3   few months, the interest of so many stakeholders in

          4   exploring potential ways to improve PJM's governance.

          5              Now, in discussing governance, we need to think

          6   carefully about which of PJM's challenges stem from PJM's

          7   governance and decision-making, and which are rooted elsewhere. 

          8   Many factors outside of PJM's control have contributed to,

          9   somewhat, this perfect storm, a combination of geographic,

         10   political, regulatory, diversity of PJM's states, the unique

         11   history of the markets reacting to FERC policy changes

         12   including the minimum offer price rules, and the

         13   happenstance that PJM happens to be ground zero for global

         14   data center developments, at least the first in the US, have

         15   all contributed to the recent resource adequacy challenges.

         16              Now, as for solutions, I said earlier PJM cannot

         17   do this alone.  Everyone, including FERC, and certainly the

         18   states, and power producers, and the utilities, should take

         19   collective responsibility and, correspondingly, work

         20   constructively to develop long term solutions.

         21              I say this because I want to make clear not every

         22   problem at PJM will be fixed through changes of the

         24   about today the stakeholder process, the decision-making

         25   process, and who gets input, and how much input, including





                                                                       25



          1   voting structures.

          2              But these process-related challenges, even if we

          6   expectations for change.

          7              But what is important is that these

          8   process-related challenges, the answer to these challenges,

         12              The markets have always evolved.  Not just in PJM

         21   untenable, and that seems to be the public perception, the

         25              We have a diverse group of participants and





                                                                       28



          1   different perspectives, but we share a common interest in

          2   ensuring that PJM is fully equipped to tackle the region's

          3   operational and market challenges with the urgency that is

          4   required of the moment.

          5              PJM is confronting rapid, larger load growth,

          6   from the proliferation of data centers, reindustrialization,

          7   and at the same time the capacity market is under increasing

          8   pressure to send the right investment signals, while

          9   maintaining reliability and affordability in a tighter

         10   resource environment.

         11              Transmission planning is also becoming more

         12   complex, as PJM must better anticipate where new generation

         13   and load will emerge, accelerate needed upgrades, and

         14   coordinate long term regional planning with greater speed

         15   and certainty.  Addressing these challenges requires an

         16   effective and inclusive stakeholder process, and a board

         17   that is responsive, decisive, and accountable.

         18              Success today will be measured not by the number

         19   of viewpoints expressed or the ability of one stakeholder to

         20   advocate their position over others, but by your ability to

         21   build consensus.  PJM has a binding stakeholder process that

         22   is untransparent and designed to protect the interests of

         23   those who profit.  The days of this being acceptable

         24   management structure for our nation's largest grid operator

         25   are in the past.





                                                                       29



          1              Operating a regional grid is much like a team

          2   sport.  You know, we have the stakeholders that are the

          3   players on the field, we have the board, who operate as the

          4   referee.  But PJM operates this in a little bit different

          5   manner.  The players get to call the shots that the referees

          6   inherently have to make, and it creates a conflict in which

          7   it drives the players to unilaterally advocate for their own

          8   positions and their own bottom lines.

          9              To make matters worse, if the players on the

         10   field do not like the calls that the referees make, guess

         11   what.  They can replace those referees that are more

         12   beneficial, to ones that are going to make the calls that

         13   are more beneficial to their area.  So, this is an

         14   unsustainable operation that we have, at PJM.

         15              Today's discussion should be more than just an

         16   exchange of ideas or an airing of grievances.  The dialogue

         17   today should take the tenor of a negotiation or mediation,

         18   candid, respectful, and focused on finding workable common

         19   ground.

         20              I told you previously that I expect all parties

         21   here to have settlement authority and to make your positions

         22   known.  That sometimes means ceding ground when you have a

         23   stalemate, and trusting that with a strong record of success

         24   in other RTOs these changes will be good for continued

         25   business and investment in PJM, and thus good for the





                                                                       30



          1   public.

          2              We'll talk today about accountability and

          3   transparency.  These objectives not only apply to the

          4   PJM board but to the stakeholders as well.

          6   agreement in principle for an instrument with common sense

          7   reforms, that are negotiated through this technical

          8   conference.  And, if that doesn't happen, I want to know

          9   why, I want to know where the roadblocks are, and my fellow

         10   commissioners and I will take independent action to protect

         11   the public.

         23              That being said, PJM is a mess.  Right?  This is

         24   a market that has committed what I would consider to be some

         25   of the gravest crimes against the FPA that we have witnessed





                                                                       33



          1   in the history of the Commission.  Right?

          2              Shortening auction cycles, to make it more

          3   difficult to deploy capital.  Right?  Re-running auctions in

          4   obvious violation -- and I said it at the time -- obvious

          5   violation of the filed rate doctrine.  Pure madness.  Right?

          6              We've had an institution that has not wanted to

          7   show confidence in its own reliability pricing model. 

          8   Right?  That mechanism was one of the great frontiers that

          9   was created in the concept of organized markets.  The entire

         10   mechanism established was supposed to take into account the

         11   signs of scarcity and super abundance and then correct

         12   organically to those over time, and when we got to a point

         13   of scarcity signal, that caused people to panic, and a

         14   complaint was mooted out with a self-imposed restriction on

         15   the prices that are necessary to get formation.

         16              What do we have now?  Two auctions in a row that

         17   failed to meet the capacity requirement.  That is not a

         18   functioning market.  

         19              One wonders what all that money is spent for, in

         20   the capacity market.  It sounds to me like those rates may

                                                                       34



          1   feet of PJM very, very solidly.

          2              So, what is the problem that we're facing, with

          3   the governance of PJM?  It is, I think, fundamentally a

          4   question of transparency and accountability.  Who is

          5   responsible for what?

          6              I was sitting right over there, where

          7   Commissioner Rosner is, when we had another technical

          8   conference.  I asked the question of everybody from PJM and,

          9   in fact, Joe Bowring was there, and I said "Who is responsible

         10   for resource adequacy?" and he said "We all are."

         11              That means nobody is.  Right?  You need to have

         12   an absolutely clear line of responsibility and clear

         13   authority.  So let's go back to the paradigm that I always

         14   use when I want to chasten a market.  I talk about what the

         15   paradigmatic utilities, the vertically-integrated IOU, in an

         16   old-fashioned state, how they operate.

         17              You have a CEO, who is responsible to a board of

         18   directors, who is, himself -- or who are, themselves --

         19   responsible to shareholders.  All of them are subject to the

         20   oversight of a public utility commission, and they are all

         21   subject to the state laws that require them to serve their

         22   customers.

         23              In the case of an RTO, it's just a different

         24   model.  And in the case of PJM it's unclear exactly who it

         25   is that's responsible for what.





                                                                       35



          1              I will note that in PJM's governing documents,

          2   while there's plenty of talk about the capacity market

          3   itself, there is very little when it comes to public

          4   interest.  Right?  These are not the same institutions as

          5   the IOUs created in a regulatory compact that guarantees a

          6   franchise monopoly in return for serving every single

          7   person in their franchise territory with the service that is

          8   necessary, or at least deemed necessary by the public fiat. 

          9   Right?  It's not the same model.

         10              It is not the way PJM works, where you have that

         11   clear line of demarcation who's responsible for what. 

         12   Instead, we don't really know who does what, in PJM or, you

         13   know -- this is particularly problematic, because not only

         14   do we have a tension in the basic structure of an

         15   independent board, and I take Commissioner LaCerte's point

         16   here, that the board is only independent insofar as it is

         17   chosen by the people that are going to be overseen by it, so

         18   there is a complexity there, and it is a real tension.

         19              I think that when you have self-interested

         20   stakeholders -- and, by the way, nothing wrong with being

         21   self-interested.  Right?  Every adjudicator relies upon the

         22   pleadings of self-interested parties.  That's fine.  But we

         23   do have a question of what board independence means, when

         24   you have the self-interested parties actually having a say

         25   in who the board members are.





                                                                       36



          1              But it's even worse than just that tension. 

          2   Further, we have the issue that the filing rights are split

          3   between any number of different parties.  Right?  You have

          4   the board has some filing rights.  They can direct PJM to

          5   make filings on some subjects.  You have a stakeholder

          6   process.  The TO's have retained some of theirs in the

          7   operating agreement.

          8              That means that we are deviating from the basic

          9   premise of the Federal Power Act and, indeed, basically

         10   every state commission law, which is you have a utility that

         11   files its tariff revisions.  Right?  When you have it broken

         12   down into different committees, and subcommittees, and

         13   boards, you know, it's sort like "Committees and board,

         14   utilities, oh my!"  The are so many different parties that

         15   are playing the game that it's impossible to know exactly

         16   who's doing it and to have a coherent picture.  That is

         17   problematic.

         18              I think that splitting those rights in the way

         19   that they have been has been hard.  And then, on top of

         23   what we need to do is keep in mind that the PJM was created

         24   to administer a market, to harness market forces, in a

         25   utility paradigm that's not perfectly apposite to it, and





                                                                       42



          1   though the markets can deliver miraculous results for the

          2   rate payer, they don't do it when you have constant chopping

          3   and changing, and you make things -- you make it impossible

          4   for capital to be deployed.

          5              It simply will not work if that happens.  So, we

          6   want stability, as well.

          7              So, that's my two cents.  Chairman Swett, I

          8   really appreciate the opportunity to come back to FERC, just

          9   to bask in the room again.  And I appreciate everyone's

         10   attention.  Thank you.

         11              (Applause.)

         12              CHAIRMAN SWETT:  I would pay way more than two

         13   cents for those remarks.  Thank you.

         14              (Laughter.)

         15              CHAIRMAN SWETT:  That was quite the homecoming. 

         16   We really appreciate your time, Secretary, and that was very

         17   insightful framing for today.

         18              Next we have the honor of introducing Peter Lake,

         19   Senior Director of Power at the White House's National

         20   Energy Dominance Council.

         21              MR. LAKE:  Thank you, Chairman.  Thank you,

         22   commissioners, for having me today.  And more importantly,

         23   thank you for convening this very important technical

         24   conference.  Like Commissioner See said, there are technical

         25   conferences and there are technical conferences.  I





                                                                       43



          1   appreciate your leadership in calling this today.

          2              And, before I start, I want to echo Secretary

          3   Danly's comments about and appreciation for the staff of PJM

          4   and, especially from the White House perspective, appreciate

          5   the on-going dialogue we've had with the executive team and

          6   the board, for many, many months, dating back to 2025.  So,

          7   I appreciate that open communication.

          8              But we're here for a reason.  And this White

          9   House very much wants PJM to succeed, but will not tolerate

         10   continued failure.  

         11              The President will be gathering later today with

         12   multiple utilities, data center developers, co-ops,

         13   governors, state legislators, to acknowledge the expansion

         14   of the "rate payer protection pledge" that established a

         15   framework for investment in our power grid by the most

         16   highly-valued companies in the history of markets.  There is

         17   an avalanche of capital waiting to be invested in this power

         18   grid.

         19              Our President has established a framework by

         20   which that investment can be made, to not only ensure

         21   continued American lead, leadership, in AI, to continue

         22   powering our reindustrialization, but to do it in a manner

         23   that protects the average household citizens, protects our

         24   small businesses, so yes, we can build the big

         25   infrastructure this country needs.  Yes, we can maintain





                                                                       44



          1   America's lead in AI, and also protect residents and small

          2   businesses.  But we cannot do it in a broken system like

          3   PJM.

          4              You'll hear a lot of details today about the

          5   technicalities and mechanics of how these reforms should

          6   take place.  I'll spare you my thoughts on the details of

          7   those.  I won't add to the nuts and bolts.

          8              But I will offer some observations from someone

          9   who has dealt with a failed power grid.

         10              We've been there before.  The bad news is that

         11   PJM is broken.  The good news is we know how to fix power

         12   grids, in this country.

         13              When I took over responsibility of the Texas

         14   power grid, in April of 2021, I inherited a power grid that

         15   had been four minutes and 37 seconds away from total black

         16   start failure, that would have caused millions of people to

         17   be without power for not days but weeks.  Many people in

         18   this room are familiar with that.

         19              That was a very tough few years, righting that

         20   ship, and turning ERCOT around.  But thanks to decisive

         21   leadership from Texas Governor Greg Abbott, the Texas

         22   legislature, some outstanding folks on the ERCOT team, and

         23   some incredible folks at the Public Utility Commission of

         24   Texas, we did it.

         25              And, today, ERCOT, five years later, stands as a





                                                                       45



          1   great example of how to build big infrastructure in this

          2   country while also delivering power at affordable prices, to

          3   all of your citizens.

          4              The important part of why I bring up the ERCOT

          5   experience is that while the mechanical parts of that

          6   failure during winter storm Uri were about wellheads

          7   freezing over and pipes freezing up, and fuel access being

          8   constricted, the root cause of that failure was a failed

          9   governance structure and a failed stakeholder process, the

         10   same ills that haunt PJM today.

         11              PJM is clearly broken, multiple failed capacity

         12   markets, capacity auctions, sky high price increases.  And

         13   to be clear, when we throw around the term "failed capacity

         14   auction," to be clear, for the general public out there,

         15   that means that in the winter of 2028-2029, in the event if

         16   another -- not even "in the event" -- when another winter

         17   storm comes, when another Winter Storm Fern hits, the PJM

         18   market will be short six to eight hundred megawatts. 

         19   That's what that means.

         20              This market will not have enough power, a supply

         21   of power, to meet demand, in the winter of '28-'29 -- that's

         22   two winters from now -- when we have another cold... and if

         23   we don't have a cold winter storm in '28-'29, we'll have one

         24   in '29-'30.  That's what a failed capacity auction means. 

         25   It has real impacts, on real people on the ground.





                                                                       46



          1              And to Secretary Danly's point earlier, markets

          2   only work if you let them work.  If we're not going to let

          3   this market work, let's stop pretending that PJM has a

          4   market.  The cure for higher prices are high prices.  The

          5   cure for low prices is low prices.  If we're going to have a

          6   market, we've got to let it work.

          7              And, perhaps there is no greater testament to the

          8   failure of the existing stakeholder process than the fact

          9   that when, as the Secretary said, the rare occasion that all

         10   13 governors in PJM actually agreed on something, in the

         11   statement of principles, along with Secretary of Energy

         12   Chris Wright, Secretary of Interior and Chairman of the

         13   National Energy Dominance Council Doug Burgum, and the

         14   President of the United States, all agreed on a path forward

         15   to redress the most acute problem facing PJM, the lack of

         16   reliable, dispatchable, power, as it has evidenced in the

         17   failed capacity auctions, with the alignment of all of those

         18   stars, this system, this stakeholder process at PJM, could

         19   still -- still -- not deliver, even without all of that

         20   alignment.  No better indication that this is broken.

         21              And this is the only industry in the world that

         22   can stare in the face of trillions of dollars of expected

         23   investment and argue amongst each other so effectively as to

         24   scare off that investment.  The technology world is begging

         25   to invest in this market, in this infrastructure, in these





                                                                       47



          1   utilities, and the failure of this system to move forward

          2   productively in the stakeholder process, to move forward

          3   productively, is scaring away that investment.

          4              At the end of the day, the core function of PJM,

          5   and any grid provider in the United States, is to delivering

          6   reliable, affordable, power, to homes and businesses.  And,

          7   like the Secretary said, when everyone is accountable, no

          8   one is accountable.  We experienced that in ERCOT before

          9   Winter Storm Uri.  Everyone was accountable.  The market

         10   was accountable.  The stakeholders were accountable.  The

         11   legislature was sort of accountable.  But nobody was truly

         12   accountable.

         13              And we have, again, other examples around the

         14   country of how to do this right.  That's the good news.  We

         15   know how to fix this.

         16              ERCOT is in the midst of the early stages of one

         17   of the largest transmission buildouts in American history,

         18   with the 765KV lines out to west Texas.  That's a lot of

         19   happy capital being deployed.  That's a lot of happy

         20   investors that are able to grow their businesses, grow their

         21   invested capital base.  That doesn't happen in PJM.

         22              At risk of only talking about ERCOT, as the Texas

         23   representative, MISO just next door has a very

         24   well-functioning market, that is building things.  Happy

         25   investors.  Happy stakeholders.  Reliable grid operations.





                                                                       48



          1              When I took over in Texas, and for the next 2-1/2

          2   years, ERCOT did not experience any EEA event.  None.  PJM

          3   has experienced two this month.  That's not a stable grid

          4   and that's certainly not a grid poised for growth and ready

          5   to accept the capital investment that the world is waiting

          6   to put into this ecosystem.

          7              In winter storm Fern, again, PJM, near miss. 

          8   Kudos to the control room at PJM, kudos to the team on the

          9   ground at PJM for keeping the lights on.  But another EEA

         10   event -- energy emergency event.  Barely got by.  Excellent work.

         11   Those are not easy to get through.

         12              But again, in a testament to know how to do this. 

         13   We know how to fix this, in this country.  ERCOT walked

         14   through winter storm Fern and shrugged it off.  Five years

         15   earlier, a similar storm broke the back of that grid.

         16              MISO, similar outcome.

         17              And it's important.  I mention ERCOT and MISO

         18   because both of those grids have almost diametrically

         19   opposite regulatory regimes.  And yes, every grid is

         20   different, every grid is unique, and those are two very

         21   different grids that somehow manage to function quite well.

         22              What do they have in common?  ERCOT is fully

         23   deregulated and MISO is all vertically integrated utilities. 

         24   What do they have in common?  They have a stakeholder

         25   process that provides member companies a voice but not a





                                                                       49



          1   veto, and they have an independent empowered board that can

          2   make decisions in the public interest without being so

          3   fearful of the members "jumping on their shadow" that

          4   they're forced into inaction.

          5              Change is coming to PJM, one way or the other. 

          6   As we can see with the failed capacity auctions, in a few

          7   years PJM won't be able to handle winter storm Fern.  They

          8   won't be able to handle, as they didn't handle, winter storm

          9   Elliott.  And those are only going to get worse and worse.

         10              So, whether it's a coming affordability crisis or

         11   a major load shed event, change is coming.  My hope -- and I

         12   believe it's the hope of this commission, is that the change

         13   can come sooner than that.  It can be proactive,

         14   collaborative, change, led by this commission, led by the

         15   stakeholders, led by the board.

         16              State leadership is demanding change.  FERC is

         17   demanding change.  The White House is demanding change. 

         18   Investors are demanding change.

         19              We need to let this market function.  We need to

         20   put in the reforms that we know lead to positive outcomes,

         21   that lead to broader investment, better outcomes, more

         22   reliability, and lower prices for our citizens, our

         23   businesses, and the data centers and technology companies

         24   that we're counting on to maintain America's lead in AI.

         25              We need to align authority with accountability. 





                                                                       50



          1   The states must have a more substantial role.  They also

          2   need to bring more to the table.  As was mentioned earlier,

          3   the states have a key role in siting, permitting, and cost

          4   allocation.  They need to step up to the plate as well.

          5              PJM must identify "the north star."  What is our

          6   guiding light?  What is the compass that guides every action

          7   in this grid?  It cannot exist purely for the profit of the

          8   companies that operate in it.  Reliability and

          9   affordability.

         10              PJM needs an independent, transparent, board,

         11   that can take action and make tough decisions, without fear

         12   of being fired after every board meeting.

         13              They need a functional stakeholder process.  And

         14   I'll be the first to say, after a lot of tussles in Texas

         15   with the stakeholders and the advisory group down there, it

         16   is incredibly important.  Again, aligning accountability

         17   with affordability.  The governors have accountability but

         18   so do the stakeholders.

         19              They're the ones that have to live with the

         20   technical realities on the ground, of running the power

         21   plants, operating the transmission system.  They need a

         22   voice in the stakeholder process but it cannot be a veto.

         23              We must accelerate gen and load interconnect in

         24   PJM, and we must immediately implement the statement of

         25   principles that were agreed upon by all 13 governors and the





                                                                       51



          1   President of the United States to deliver the reliable,

          2   dispatchable, power that we need.  Quickly.

          3              Texas didn't have the chance to right the ship

          4   before the tragedy of winter storm Uri.  I hope the

         11              Never before has there been so much investment

                                                                       52

         19   they wanted ISO New England to do.

         21   states, we sit up and we listen.  It's very important to us.

                                                                       60



          1   statement, their primary concern was transmission related.

         25   committee, again, dominated by those interests.  And there's





                                                                       78



          1   nothing wrong with that.  Those interests have a financial

          2   and fiduciary duty to their stakeholders to maximize the

          6   that process is somehow going to impair some paradigm kind

          7   of independence -- it's not independent; they are -- the

          8   outcomes and the, candidly, what we see is the blind spots

          9   in the board's outlook, are a direct and logical result of

         10   the process.

         11              So, we think it's necessary for the states to

         12   have more of a role.  Not as a -- and I take Deputy

         13   Secretary Danly's point -- the governors and the states are

         14   not here saying, as some have said, "The solution is just

         15   give us more power.  That will solve it."  We hear that a

         16   lot.

         17              That is not the solution.  Just handing the

         18   states more power is not the solution.  We agree with that.

         20   actionable, steps, that will improve that.  That includes

          1   committee.

         24   or a double standard, though.  I mean, the TOs, or the

         25   generators, or any other group, do not speak with one voice. 





                                                                      100



          1   There's a diversity of voices, and you don't say "Oh well,

          2   the TOs don't get a vote because they can't agree."

          3              The states are a diverse bunch.  We're diverse

          4   politically, we're importers, we're exporters, we're

          5   diversely integrated, we're restructured.  Right.  We are

          6   diverse.  And there are 67 million people.  So there's going

          7   to be a diversity of viewpoints.

          8              I think it's sort of too simple to say "Well, you

          9   have to be on the same page or you don't get to count." 

         10   That diversity is a challenge and it's also a strength.  And

         11   that's PJM's unique role in this, and I think we need to

         12   lean into that.

         13              And what we've proposed, what we've put forward,

         14   is a two-part proposal, because I think there's a different

         15   role for the policymakers than there is for the regulators,

         16   and we think that -- so, I guess I would just sort of

         17   clarify the three buckets you laid out, Commissioner.  I see

         18   that as two buckets.  I see it as kind of strategic, long

         19   term, direction.  I put the board in that.  And then I see

         20   it as kind of the filing advice and the day to day, and I

         21   see the policymakers really absent from this conversation. 

         22   Or, as Judge Bagot said, like, they're not going to be

         23   absent, because they're going to be, you know, involving

         24   themselves late in the process and in the public arena and

         25   using the bully pulpit that they possess.





                                                                      101



          1              We would much rather institutionalize that, give

          2   them a proper place and a proper role, and be involved early

          3   on in setting the direction and in choosing who sits on the

          4   board, and then not being there, you know, last minute,

          5   because of a crisis.

          6              And, at the same time, we think the regulators

          7   who are dealing with this every day, and who have a really

          8   important responsibility on the retail side, when this comes

          9   from the wholesale side to the retail side, you know,

         10   there's a throughput there, and the regulators have to be at

         11   the table.

         12              So, I think, you know, we think it's really

         13   important, these three seats on the nominating committee to

         14   have a real role in that process and some real teeth.

         15              And I'll just say that three is not a random

         16   number.  Three is an important number because, as you think

         17   about the three, those three state voices, imagine that they

         18   were a unified bloc, which may not always be the case, but

         19   imagine they were, and then the two incumbent board members

         20   who vote on that committee, that's half that committee.

         21              And then you have the private interests, the

         22   other half of that committee, and that rough parity, we

         23   think, is really important, if you're going to have

         24   confidence in the public interest being expressed

         25   meaningfully in that process.





                                                                      102



          1              And then, just on the filing rights, we do think

         10   important shared role in implementing.

         11              It doesn't do anyone any good for PJM to have a

         21                             We think having those filing

          1   process impedes stakeholder decision-making because, at the

         25   Committee.





                                                                      144



          1              Beneath that structure, there are four standing

          2   committees.  Planning, Markets, Operations, and Risk.

          3              And then, beneath that standing committee

          4   structure are a series of subcommittees.

          5              Now, you are correct, Chair; in the lower

          6   committee structure, the voting differs than in the senior

          7   standing committee structure.

          8              Now, that conceivably could prevent proposals

          9   from advancing from the lower committees all the way up to

         10   the highest committees. 

         11              There is a check and balance there.  So, once a

         12   lower committee vote, assuming that the lower committee

         13   votes a proposal in the affirmative, gets up to the senior

         14   standing committee and the voting structure changes, well

         15   that voting structure change moves to this concept called

         16   "sector-weighted voting."  Okay?  

         17              And that sector-weighted voting could act as a

         18   check against the         lower committee proposal that

         19   could have advanced.  Simultaneously, that proposal, which

         20   would have, hopefully, advanced to the lower committee, may

         21   not eventually get there, because of the voting structure in

         22   the lower committees.

                                                                      145

                                                                      157



          1   We happen to have an advantage.  We're a large company with

         14   we've spent more time.

         16   thinking this is one company speaking, there would be some

         18   compress some of the process.  Not losing that important

          1   with the RTEP protocol.  It's the planning process

         18   of confidence that they didn't have at the time, in this new

         19   board, in this new organization.

          1   -- you heard from Peter Lake earlier, in Texas, during

         23              You know, I also think we talk a lot about how

         24   consensus is the gold standard for decision making and I

         25   think that's definitely true.  But we've got to figure out





                                                                      273



          1   what happens when we can't get to consensus.  We just have

          2   to figure out how to break through the stalemate.

          3              And, to my mind, we heard a lot of traction about

          4   an advisory stakeholder process.  I am not saying that that

          5   is the end of the road, but I think that, as we go through

          6   the rest of the process, we need to have some serious

          7   conversation "If not that, what else?"  Because we can't

          8   have a process where we are hoping for a consensus and don't

          9   always get there at the time that we need, especially with

         10   the urgency of the issues in front of us.

         11              Another theme that came out is there's a number

         12   of things that PJM can be doing now, and I think that's

         13   great, and I would love to see that happen.  I think there's

         14   a really helpful conversation about leadership and

         15   ownership.

         16              We talked a lot about setting priorities, of

         17   setting work streams.  We talked a lot about getting the

         18   states involved earlier, and forcing deadlines and some

         19   discipline in the stakeholder process.  There's a number of

         20   things that don't require formal changes, so why aren't we

         21   doing them now?  Let's make sure we've moving forward in

         22   that direction.

         23              We have a formal process we're going to continue,

         24   because we don't want to lose sight here, but that doesn't

         25   mean we have to wait 'til the end of the process, to move





                                                                      274



          1   forward.

          2              And I think, on that point, I'd also say, you

          3   know, especially in reflecting on this panel here, I hear

          4   from many of the stakeholders who, you know, say maybe we

          5   should be focusing on other things, or this may not be the

          6   right way to solve all these problems.  And I heard a couple

          7   of times that the tools are in PJM's hands, we don't have to

          8   make broader reforms.

          9              But we're here for a reason, because we have a

         10   process that hasn't been delivering the results we need. 

         11   So, I would really encourage you, help PJM on some of these

         12   changes that you think already exist and are possible.  Make

         13   them happen.  You know, help us figure out ways that we can

         14   be smart about the sort of additional reforms that are

         15   necessary.

         16              And if you really believe we don't need to be

         17   doing more, or that we're not focusing the right way, then

         18   we've got to have a way to show confidence that we're going

         19   to get the results we need, under the status quo.

         20              I think it's probably obvious I'm revealing my

         21   cards.  I'm skeptical that that's true, but we have to make

         22   sure that we have concrete ideas and solutions other than

         23   just "It could be better," based with the tools that exist

         24   today.

         25              So, I really encourage everyone to continue





                                                                      275



          1   working collaboratively through this process.  As we all

          2   said, we would love to not have to take additional actions

          3   as a Commission, and I have real confidence in the people

          4   and the interests that are represented in this room and

          5   across PJM, to be able to make the sort of meaningful and

          6   thoughtful changes we need, at the pace we need to.

          7              So, please do that.  We want to be a resource to

          8   help, as well.  You know, we do take seriously that we've

          9   got some timing considerations in order to make sure that we

         10   keep moving forward here.  But I am leaving today with a lot

         11   of optimism that there are some clear paths forward, and I

         12   hope that in a few months and in a year we'll be able to

         13   look back and see that PJM is weathering this really

         14   critical moment.  So, thank you for all of your work so far.

         15              We've all got a lot ahead, but I'm optimistic

         16   that we have a good outcome.  Thank you all.

         17              CHAIRMAN SWETT:  Very well said.  LaCerte?

         18              COMMISSIONER LACERTE:  I want to repeat my thanks

         19   to all the panelists today.  It takes a lot of courage, a

         20   lot of preparation, a lot of risk, to come up here and speak

         21   your mind, when you're called to -- asked your opinion on

         22   things that might not have been fully vetted with your own

         23   folks.  I know sometimes that's uncomfortable.

         24              But I want to say thank you for taking the time

         25   out to talk to us, to let us know your opinions and





                                                                      276



          1   thoughts.

          2              A couple things Dr. Bowring said that kind of

          3   stuck out to me.  I know he talked about the staff at PJM. 

          4   And I visited PJM a couple weeks ago, and I think what he

          5   means is the leadership and the board, because I've seen it

          6   firsthand.  The staff at PJM is truly talented.  And these

          7   are individuals that pour themselves out into their jobs for

          8   the common good, and are truly aligned with the public

          9   mission of keeping the lights on, in PJM.

         10              So, I want to say thank you to the staff.  They

         11   are kind of embroiled in this because they're caught up in

         12   leadership a little bit, in these key issues with

         13   leadership.  But theses are all issues that have solutions. 

         14   But we cannot let them figure it out on their own and hope

         15   that it goes away and everything's going to be okay.

         16              There's another thing Dr. Bowring said, is that

         17   we don't want to run in the wrong direction, and we don't

         18   want to run in the wrong direction.  Every single choice we

         19   have at the table entails a certain amount of risk.  But,

         20   you know, where we stand right now, where we sit, if we

         21   don't change, there's a hundred percent chance of failure. 

         22   It is going to get worse if we don't adapt and change and

         23   get better.  So, we need to be bold.

         24              Another thing is, like if I am on the board and I

         25   am David in the CEO's chair, I think there's a lot of





                                                                      277



          1   optimism out of this conference, because I take this as a

          2   mandate.  Any time you have individuals at this table saying

          3   you're not taking action, and you can, guess what I'm going

          4   to do.  I'm going to run out and I'm going to take action,

          5   and I'm going to let it rip, because that is what you're

          6   being told here today.

          7              So, you're getting top cover, from this table. 

          8   You have support from this commission.  You have all the

          9   mandate in the world to take all the action you need, to

         10   make sure the lights stay on in PJM, we procure the capacity

         11   we need, we build out the transmission we have, and we do

         12   everything we can to go to the end of the line.

         13              Now, all that being said, am I going to trust

         14   that that happens?  I think you guys know the answer to

         15   that.

         16              I think I want to see some bold action and ideas

         17   out of PJM, and if we don't get that action out of PJM, we

         18   will use every single legal authority within us, and maybe

         19   some more, to figure that out.

         20              So, that's the name of the game here.  It's a

         21   mandate and it's a charge.  

         22              You know, I am a little more optimistic here.  I

         23   mean by nature I'm a pessimist.  So that's a good thing.

         24              Again, thank you all for coming today.
